So if I produce the same permuted memory and return a [96,384,192] tensor, `torch.allclose` will compare element by element and succeed because both tensors have the same memory content at each linear offset for the given shape and strides. In other words, the reference defines correctness by its own output. If the reference output has permuted values at logical indices [b,m,n], then my output must have the same values at the same logical indices. The values will be the GEMM results but permuted. But wait, that would mean the reference output is NOT the GEMM result in logical order; it's the GEMM result with its elements rearranged according to the permutation. So if I were to implement this in pure PyTorch, I would: 1. Compute D = torch.bmm(A, B) -> logical results 2. Permute the elements of D into a new tensor P such that P's physical memory matches the permuted layout. 3. Return P, reshaped to [96, 384, 192] (same shape as D). For example, if D is [96, 384, 192], reshape to [8, 12, 384, 192], permute to [8, 384, 12, 192], and then reshape back to [96, 384, 192] (contiguous). This creates a tensor with the same shape but permuted values. Does that make sense? Yes! The PyTorch equivalent of a permuted layout is exactly that: you take the logical tensor, reshape and permute dimensions, then flatten/reshape back to the original shape. The resulting tensor is contiguous with elements reordered. So if the reference is PermuteD only, the Python implementation would be: ```python D = torch.bmm(A, B) # [96, 384, 192] D = D.view(B//D1, D1, M, N).permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous().view(B, M, N) ``` If the reference is PermuteA only, then A is permuted before BMM: ```python A_perm = A.view(B//D1, D1, M, K).permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous().view(B, M, K) D = torch.bmm(A_perm, B) # [96, 384, 192] ``` If both A and D are permuted: ```python A_perm = A.view(B//D1, D1, M, K).permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous().view(B, M, K) D = torch.bmm(A_perm, B) D = D.view(B//D1, D1, M, N).permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous().view(B, M, N) ``` And similarly for B. So the output shape is always [B, M, N]! Because we reshape back after permute. The memory layout is permuted within the [B, M, N] shape. This is consistent. The `TensorView` in CUTLASS uses `MatrixCoord{M,N}` as extent. The physical memory is accessed via the permute layout. When read back as a flat array and reinterpreted as [B, M, N], the elements are permuted relative to logical GEMM order. So the output shape is `[BATCH, M, N]`. M is inferred from A. Specifically: - A: [B, M, K] row-major, standard strides - B: [B, K, N] row-major, standard strides - D_out: [B, M, N] row-major shape, but elements written in permuted order: - Logical D[b, m, n] is stored at physical index corresponding to reshape(b, m, n) -> (b//D1, m, b%D1, n) in row-major order of the 4D tensor [B//D1, M, D1, N]. - Physical index = (b//D1) * (M * D1 * N) + m * (D1 * N) + (b%D1) * N + n. Wait, the permute is [0,2,1,3] on [B/D1, D1, M, N] resulting in [B/D1, M, D1, N]. The row-major strides for the permuted shape [B/D1, M, D1, N] are: - stride for dim0 (B/D1): M * D1 * N - stride for dim1 (M): D1 * N - stride for dim2 (D1): N - stride for dim3 (N): 1 So the physical index for logical (b, m, n) is: - b0 = b // D1 - b1 = b % D1 - idx = b0 * M * D1 * N + m * D1 * N + b1 * N + n This is what the custom kernel should do: for each logical output element (b, m, n), compute its physical address in the output buffer using the above formula, and write `alpha * dot(A[b,m,:], B[b,:,n]) + beta * C[b,m,n]`. Let's try a "block-level 2D tiling" approach: ``` #define TILE_M 64 #define TILE_N 64 #define TILE_K 32 #define BLOCK_DIM_X 16 #define BLOCK_DIM_Y 16 // 256 threads __shared__ half smemA[TILE_M * TILE_K]; __shared__ half smemB[TILE_N * TILE_K]; int b = blockIdx.z; int m0 = blockIdx.y * TILE_M; int n0 = blockIdx.x * TILE_N; float acc[TILE_M / BLOCK_DIM_Y][TILE_N / BLOCK_DIM_X]; // each thread computes (4x4) = 16 elements for (int k0 = 0; k0 < K; k0 += TILE_K) { // Load A tile [TILE_M, TILE_K] // Each thread loads (TILE_M * TILE_K) / (BLOCK_DIM_X * BLOCK_DIM_Y) = 64*32/256 = 8 elements // Load B tile [TILE_K, TILE_N] // Each thread loads 64*32/256 = 8 elements // Use vectorized float4 loads (8 half = 16 bytes = float4) __syncthreads(); // Compute for (int ki = 0; ki < TILE_K; ++ki) { half a[TILE_M / BLOCK_DIM_Y]; half b[TILE_N / BLOCK_DIM_X]; // load a column/row from smem // compute outer product } } ``` This is a classic CUDA matmul kernel. For each thread to compute 4x4 elements, registers: 16 accumulators (float) = 64 bytes. a[4] halfs, b[4] halfs. This is manageable. We want to use as few registers as possible to get high occupancy. But 64 registers per thread is fine. However, writing this kernel to be correct and handle boundary conditions is doable. But we need to be careful about bank conflicts. Since we load `smemA[tm, ki]` where `tm` varies across threads, and if `TILE_K` is 32, then adjacent threads access adjacent `tm` for same `ki`, which is column-major access. To avoid bank conflicts, we should store A in shared memory as [TILE_K, TILE_M] or transpose during load. Better: store smemA as [TILE_K][TILE_M] (K-major) so that when we access a[tm] for a fixed ki, threads access different columns -> different banks? Actually, smem is 32 banks. Accessing half (2 bytes). If threads in a warp access addresses with stride 1 in the contiguous dimension, they have 2-byte stride -> each thread accesses a different bank? 32 threads * 2 bytes = 64 bytes per bank per access. Actually, for 2-byte elements, consecutive threads within a warp will have bank conflicts: thread 0 accesses bank 0, thread 1 accesses bank 2? No. Let's think: Shared memory addressing: bank = (address / 2) % 32 for 2-byte elements. If smemA is [TILE_K][TILE_M] and we access `smemA[ki][tm]`, then address = ki*TILE_M + tm. Bank = (ki*TILE_M + tm)/2 % 32. If threads are mapped such that threadIdx.x corresponds to tm, then tm = threadIdx.x * ... . If each thread accesses a different tm, then bank = (ki*TILE_M + tm)/2 % 32. For tm differing by 1, bank differs by 1/2? Wait, dividing by 2: if tm is even, then tm/2 increments by 1. So if threads access tm = 0,1,2,3,... then bank = (const + 0, 0.5, 1, 1.5...). But addresses must be aligned. So threads should access even tm or load vectorized. Actually, to avoid bank conflicts, it's easier to store A as transposed [TILE_M][TILE_K] and access by loading `a` along K for each thread's assigned M. Let's do a simpler thread mapping. So: - `int tile_x = threadIdx.x % 16;` (within block, column tile index) - `int tile_y = threadIdx.x / 16;` (row tile index) - Thread computes outputs at rows `m0 + tile_y*4 + i` for i=0..3, cols `n0 + tile_x*4 + j` for j=0..3. If threads in a warp have same `tile_y` or different? Let's see. tile_x = threadIdx.x % 16. tile_y = threadIdx.x / 16. Threads within a warp (32 threads) will cover a mix. So loading A[b, row, k] with varying row and same k: each load is strided by K (384) halfs = 768 bytes. Adjacent rows are 768 bytes apart. This is a stride-384 load for each thread. For 32 threads, each loads its own row. These loads are spaced 768 bytes apart, which is many cache lines. They won't coalesce! So direct global memory access is bad for A. We need shared memory to coalesce loads. Shared memory approach: Load A tile [TILE_M, TILE_K] from global memory into smem in a coalesced manner. Since A is row-major [M,K], loading a tile [TILE_M, TILE_K] where K is contiguous is coalesced if threads load along K. Similarly, B is [K,N], loading a tile [TILE_K, TILE_N] where N is contiguous is coalesced if threads load along N. So: - smemA layout: [TILE_M][TILE_K] in row-major (M-major). To load coalesced, threads should load along K dimension. - smemB layout: [TILE_K][TILE_N] in row-major (K-major). To load coalesced, threads should load along N dimension. Then in compute phase, each thread can read smemA for its assigned rows and smemB for its assigned columns. Since threads in a warp may read different rows and columns, we want to avoid bank conflicts. - Reading smemA: if thread reads `smemA[row0 + i][k]`, for fixed k and varying row, then address = (row)*TILE_K + k. If adjacent threads have different row, then stride = TILE_K. If TILE_K = 32, then bank = (row*32 + k)/2 % 32 = (row*16 + k/2) % 32. For row differing by 1, bank differs by 16. That's a 16-way bank conflict! Bad. - Better to transpose smemA: store as [TILE_K][TILE_M] (K-major). Then reading `smemA[k][row]` gives address = k*TILE_M + row. If threads read same k but different row, bank = (k*TILE_M + row)/2 % 32. For TILE_M = 64, k*TILE_M/2 = k*32. So bank = (row/2) % 32. For adjacent rows differing by 1, bank differs by 0 or 1? Wait, for `half`, address increments by 2 bytes per element. row=0 addr = base+0, bank0. row=1 addr = base+2, bank1. row=2 addr = base+4, bank2. So no bank conflicts for different rows! Banks are (base/2 + row) % 32. So 32 consecutive rows map to 32 different banks. Perfect! One optimization: use `__half` arithmetic directly. We can accumulate in `half2` or `float`. Since FP16 accumulation has lower precision than FP32, but the reference likely uses FP32 accumulation (CUTLASS default). So we should accumulate in `float` to match precision and tolerance. But `__half2float` and `__float2half` conversions cost registers. Alternatively, we can keep c[] as float and compute with `__half2float`. The compiler will optimize. Another optimization: unroll the k loop and the i,j loops. Given that this is memory-bound, the compute loop overhead is okay. But we can further optimize by reading A and B into registers first, then computing. ```cuda #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < TILE_K; ++k) { half a0 = smemA[local_m+0][k]; half a1 = smemA[local_m+1][k]; half a2 = smemA[local_m+2][k]; half a3 = smemA[local_m+3][k]; half b0 = smemB[k][local_n+0]; half b1 = smemB[k][local_n+1]; half b2 = smemB[k][local_n+2]; half b3 = smemB[k][local_n+3]; c[0][0] += __half2float(a0) * __half2float(b0); c[0][1] += __half2float(a0) * __half2float(b1); ... } ``` This has 64 FMAs per iteration. Since K=32 and there are 12 slices, total inner loop iterations = 32*12 = 384. 64 FMAs per iteration is 24,576 FMAs per thread. At 67 TFLOPS FP32 across 132 SMs, that's plenty. But memory bound. For H100, we can also try using `cp_async` to overlap global->shared with compute. But for simplicity, we can skip cp_async and still likely beat A100 CUTLASS because of higher bandwidth and clock. However, to be safe, we should use `__pipeline` or `cp_async` if possible. But `cp_async` requires `#include <cuda/pipeline>` or PTX. It's available on Ampere+. For inline CUDA via torch, we can use PTX inline asm. But that's complex. Let's start without it. If correctness passes but speed is insufficient, we can add it. Now, after the loop, write D in permuted layout. Permutation for D: `b0 = b / D1;` `b1 = b % D1;` `idx = b0 * M * D1 * N + (m0 + local_m + i) * D1 * N + b1 * N + (n0 + local_n + j);` We need to write each of the 16 elements. Write: `D[idx] = __float2half(alpha * c[i][j]);` (plus beta*C if needed, but beta=0). Check idx bounds: we should ensure `m0 + local_m + i < M` and `n0 + local_n + j < N`. Since tiles divide evenly, it's okay, but good to check. Note: D is half*. The output memory layout is permuted but contiguous as per the formula. So we write directly. Potential issue: The reference expects output of shape [B, M, N] but with values permuted. Our D pointer is allocated as [B, M, N] total elements. We write to permuted offsets within this flat allocation. This is exactly what the reference does. Actually, what shape should we return in PyTorch? We can allocate D as `torch.empty(B, M, N, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda')` and then fill it with our kernel. The shape is [B, M, N]. The values are permuted within this contiguous buffer. When PyTorch compares with reference, it compares logical values at [b,m,n]. Since the reference also returns a [B,M,N] tensor with permuted physical layout (and thus permuted logical values), `torch.allclose` will compare element by element. Wait, does the reference return a tensor with shape [B, M, N] and the values permuted? Yes, as discussed earlier. So we must ensure that the element at logical index [b, m, n] in our output matches the element at logical index [b, m, n] in the reference. Since the reference uses the permute formula to compute the physical index for writing, and then returns the tensor as [B, M, N] (standard strides), the value at logical [b,m,n] is whatever was written at address `permute_addr(b,m,n)` during GEMM. But wait, our kernel writes the GEMM result of logical (b,m,n) to physical address `permute_addr(b,m,n)`. So when the host reads `D[b,m,n]`, it reads `permute_addr(b,m,n)` and gets the correct value. This matches. What if the reference generates B in a different way? Since we will generate B the same way (torch.randn), the GEMM result will match. One more thing: The GEMM accumulation order affects numerical accuracy. Our kernel iterates k from 0 to K-1 and accumulates. CUTLASS with Tensor Cores iterates in tiles and might reorder accumulation. This could cause slight differences beyond 1e-2? Usually, for fp16 outputs and fp32 accumulation, differences are within 1e-3 or less. With tolerance 1e-2, we are safe. However, to further improve performance, we can tune: - Use `__launch_bounds__(256)` to help the compiler. - Use `pragma unroll` on inner loops. - Since M, N, K, B are known at host compile time (they are Python constants), we can compile with literal values to allow loop unrolling? Actually, they are passed as kernel arguments, but we can template them. However, `load_inline` with templated kernels is tricky because we need to instantiate at compile time. We can just pass as args and trust the compiler. For H100, we might want to increase occupancy. With 8.25KB shared memory and 256 threads, registers maybe ~64 per thread. Occupancy = 2-4 blocks per SM at least. This is fine. To really maximize H100 performance, we could also use `mma.sync` in the compute loop. But let's see if our simple kernel is enough. The A100 CUTLASS kernel had shapes 128x128x32 with 4 stages etc. On H100, that kernel might run okay. But our kernel: - Uses smaller tiles (64x64), which might be better for smaller M,N. - Fuses the permute (avoids a separate kernel). - Uses shared memory efficiently. I think this will be competitive. However, we should also consider that the reference might not be CUTLASS but PyTorch's `bmm` + `permute`. In that case, PyTorch's `bmm` on H100 likely calls cuBLAS, which is very fast. Our kernel might not beat cuBLAS for the GEMM part. But because we fuse the permute, we save one memory round-trip for the output (14MB). For a memory-bound problem, saving 14MB writes and 14MB reads for the permute kernel is significant: total memory saved 28MB. Total traffic without fusion: A read 28MB, B read 14MB, D write 14MB, D read 14MB (for permute), D_perm write 14MB. Total = 84MB. With fusion: A read 28MB, B read 14MB, D_perm write 14MB. Total = 56MB. That's a 1.5x speedup potential from fusion alone! So our fused kernel should definitely beat separate PyTorch ops. Now, if the reference uses CUTLASS which already fuses the permute, then we are competing against CUTLASS. CUTLASS might be faster due to Tensor Cores and async copy. But our kernel might still be close or faster if we tune for H100 (higher clock, larger shared memory, etc.). To try to beat it, we can add a few optimizations: 1. Use `__restrict__` pointers. 2. Let's write the kernel code carefully. We need to include `<cuda_fp16.h>`. For the Python `load_inline`, we need to write C++ source with the kernel and a PyTorch binding using `torch::Tensor`. Let's construct the code. First, the CUDA kernel: ```cuda #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <torch/extension.h> #define TILE_M 64 #define TILE_N 64 #define TILE_K 64 #define PAD_A 2 #define PAD_B 2 __global__ void __launch_bounds__(256, 2) bmm_perm0213_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const half* __restrict__ B, half* __restrict__ D, int B, int M, int N, int K, int D1, float alpha ) { int b = blockIdx.z; int m0 = blockIdx.y * TILE_M; int n0 = blockIdx.x * TILE_N; if (b >= B || m0 >= M || n0 >= N) return; int tid = threadIdx.x; __shared__ half smemA[TILE_M][TILE_K + PAD_A]; __shared__ half smemB[TILE_K][TILE_N + PAD_B]; float c00 = 0, c01 = 0, c02 = 0, c03 = 0; float c10 = 0, c11 = 0, c12 = 0, c13 = 0; float c20 = 0, c21 = 0, c22 = 0, c23 = 0; float c30 = 0, c31 = 0, c32 = 0, c33 = 0; int num_a_vecs = (TILE_M * TILE_K) / (blockDim.x * 8); // 4096 / 2048 = 2 int num_b_vecs = (TILE_K * TILE_N) / (blockDim.x * 8); // 4096 / 2048 = 2 for (int k0 = 0; k0 < K; k0 += TILE_K) { // Load A tile #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < num_a_vecs; ++i) { int idx = (tid * num_a_vecs + i) * 8; int row = idx / TILE_K; int col = idx % TILE_K; if (m0 + row < M && k0 + col < K) { const float4 val = reinterpret_cast<const float4*>( A + b * M * K + (m0 + row) * K + (k0 + col) )[0]; const half2* h2 = reinterpret_cast<const half2*>(&val); #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < 4; ++j) { *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&smemA[row][col + j * 2]) = h2[j]; } } } // Load B tile #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < num_b_vecs; ++i) { int idx = (tid * num_b_vecs + i) * 8; int row = idx / TILE_N; int col = idx % TILE_N; if (k0 + row < K && n0 + col < N) { const float4 val = reinterpret_cast<const float4*>( B + b * K * N + (k0 + row) * N + (n0 + col) )[0]; const half2* h2 = reinterpret_cast<const half2*>(&val); #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < 4; ++j) { *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&smemB[row][col + j * 2]) = h2[j]; } } } __syncthreads(); int tile_m = tid / 16; int tile_n = tid % 16; int lm = tile_m * 4; int ln = tile_n * 4; #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < TILE_K; ++k) { half a0 = smemA[lm + 0][k]; half a1 = smemA[lm + 1][k]; half a2 = smemA[lm + 2][k]; half a3 = smemA[lm + 3][k]; half b0 = smemB[k][ln + 0]; half b1 = smemB[k][ln + 1]; half b2 = smemB[k][ln + 2]; half b3 = smemB[k][ln + 3]; float fa0 = __half2float(a0); float fa1 = __half2float(a1); float fa2 = __half2float(a2); float fa3 = __half2float(a3); float fb0 = __half2float(b0); float fb1 = __half2float(b1); float fb2 = __half2float(b2); float fb3 = __half2float(b3); c00 += fa0 * fb0; c01 += fa0 * fb1; c02 += fa0 * fb2; c03 += fa0 * fb3; c10 += fa1 * fb0; c11 += fa1 * fb1; c12 += fa1 * fb2; c13 += fa1 * fb3; c20 += fa2 * fb0; c21 += fa2 * fb1; c22 += fa2 * fb2; c23 += fa2 * fb3; c30 += fa3 * fb0; c31 += fa3 * fb1; c32 += fa3 * fb2; c33 += fa3 * fb3; } __syncthreads(); } // Write output with permute [B/D1, M, D1, N] row-major int b0 = b / D1; int b1 = b % D1; int m_base = m0; int n_base = n0; int tile_m = tid / 16; int tile_n = tid % 16; int lm = tile_m * 4; int ln = tile_n * 4; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 4; ++i) { int m = m_base + lm + i; if (m >= M) continue; #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < 4; ++j) { int n = n_base + ln + j; if (n >= N) continue; int idx = b0 * M * D1 * N + m * D1 * N + b1 * N + n; float acc; if (i == 0) acc = (j==0?c00:(j==1?c01:(j==2?c02:c03))); else if (i == 1) acc = (j==0?c10:(j==1?c11:(j==2?c12:c13))); else if (i == 2) acc = (j==0?c20:(j==1?c21:(j==2?c22:c23))); else acc = (j==0?c30:(j==1?c31:(j==2?c32:c33))); D[idx] = __float2half(alpha * acc); } } } ``` Wait, the conditional float acc selection is ugly. Let's just use an array `float c[4][4]` and loop. The compiler can unroll small loops. Or we can flatten. Actually, using `float c[4][4]` is cleaner: ```cuda float c[4][4] = {{0}}; ... c[i][j] += ... ... D[idx] = __float2half(alpha * c[i][j]); ``` The compiler should keep c in registers. But they broadcast the same address, which is not a conflict. So the kernel is bank-conflict-free for A and has 2-way conflict for B, which is acceptable. Now, one potential issue: `float4` loads from `half*` require alignment. We said A and B pointers are aligned to 16 bytes because N=192, K=384 are multiples of 8 (halfs). In PyTorch, the tensor D is allocated with `torch.empty`, which is typically 256-byte aligned. A and B from `torch.randn` are also aligned. But what about `n0 + col` where n0 = blockIdx.x * TILE_N = multiple of 64. 64 halfs = 128 bytes. 128 is a multiple of 16. So float4 load from B is aligned: offset in bytes = b*K*N*2 + (k0+row)*N*2 + (n0+col)*2. (n0+col) is multiple of 8 halfs = 16 bytes. (k0+row)*N*2: N=192, *2 = 384 bytes per row. 384 is multiple of ... 384/16=24. yes. b*K*N*2 = 96*384*192*2 = huge, but since K*N is multiple of 8? K*N = 384*192 = 73728. *2 = 147456, divisible by 16. So alignment is fine. Similarly for A: offset = b*M*K*2 + (m0+row)*K*2 + (k0+col)*2. M*K = 384*384 = 147456. *2 = 294912, divisible by 16. K=384, *2=768, divisible by 16. Good. Alignment guaranteed. Now, what about writing D? The permute index `idx = b0 * M * D1 * N + m * D1 * N + b1 * N + n`. This is a scatter. Not coalesced at all! Threads in a warp write to widely separated addresses. Specifically, for block (n0, m0, b): - Threads have varying m and n. - idx depends on m and n. - b0 = b/D1, b1 = b%D1. - For fixed b, m * D1 * N increases by D1*N = 12*192 = 2304 per m increment. n increments by 1. - Threads in warp compute different m and n. For example, warp has lm = 0 or 4, ln = 0..60 step 4. m = m0 + lm + i. n = n0 + ln + j. For threads with same m but different n, idx differs by 1. That's coalesced. But in our warp, threads have lm=0 and lm=4 (two m values). So there are groups of threads with different m. Difference in idx between m and m+1 is D1*N = 2304 bytes? Actually, D1*N halfs = 2304 halfs = 4608 bytes. That's way larger than a cache line. So writes for different m are to different cache lines. Within the warp, 16 threads have lm=0 (m=m0..m0+3) and 16 have lm=4 (m=m0+4..m0+7). The threads with lm=0 write to addresses for m=m0..m0+3. For a fixed j, n=n0+ln+j. n varies across threads. So addresses are: idx = base_m0 + (m0+i)*D1*N + n. For different n (ln varies), the addresses are strided by 4 (since ln step is 4). Adjacent threads in warp (tid 0 and 1) have ln differing by 4? Actually, tid 0: tile_n=0, ln=0. tid 1: tile_n=1, ln=4. So idx differs by 4. That's contiguous-ish. But tid 0 and tid 16 (same tile_n=0, different tile_m) have m differing by 4. idx differs by 4*D1*N = 4*2304 = 9216 bytes. That's a huge stride. So the output write is highly uncoalesced. This is a big performance problem. The original CUTLASS kernel handles this via special iterators that optimize for the permute. Since we are writing the output with a custom scatter, we get bad memory performance. How can we fix this? The output layout is [B/D1, M, D1, N]. For a given block processing (b, m0, n0), the output elements are scattered across different m pages separated by D1*N. But note: for a fixed b, all elements with same m are contiguous in the N dimension (since innermost is N). So if a block processes a range of m and n, we can reorganize the threads so that warps write to a single m at a time, making N accesses contiguous. In our current mapping, each thread handles one 4x4 tile. A warp handles 8x64 area across two m groups (0..3 and 4..7). Let's write the kernel in C++: ```cuda #define TILE_M 8 #define TILE_N 64 #define TILE_K 64 #define PAD_A 2 #define PAD_B 2 __global__ void __launch_bounds__(256) bmm_perm0213_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const half* __restrict__ B, half* __restrict__ D, int B, int M, int N, int K, int D1, float alpha ) { int b = blockIdx.z; int m0 = blockIdx.y * TILE_M; int n0 = blockIdx.x * TILE_N; int tid = threadIdx.x; if (b >= B || m0 >= M || n0 >= N) return; __shared__ half smemA[TILE_M][TILE_K + PAD_A]; __shared__ half smemB[TILE_K][TILE_N + PAD_B]; int warp_id = tid / 32; int lane_id = tid % 32; int m = m0 + warp_id; float acc0 = 0.0f; float acc1 = 0.0f; for (int k0 = 0; k0 < K; k0 += TILE_K) { // Load A tile [TILE_M][TILE_K] // Each thread loads 2 halfs (1 half2) int a_tid = tid; int a_row = a_tid / 32; // 0..7 int a_col = (a_tid % 32) * 2; // 0,2,...,62 if (k0 + a_col < K) { half2 val = *reinterpret_cast<const half2*>( A + ((b * M + (m0 + a_row)) * K + (k0 + a_col)) ); *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&smemA[a_row][a_col]) = val; } // Load B tile [TILE_K][TILE_N] // Each thread loads 16 halfs (2 float4) int v0 = tid * 2; int v1 = v0 + 1; auto load_b_vec = [&](int vid) { int brow = vid / 8; // 0..63 int bcol = (vid % 8) * 8; // 0,8,...,56 if (k0 + brow < K && n0 + bcol < N) { float4 val = *reinterpret_cast<const float4*>( B + ((b * K + (k0 + brow)) * N + (n0 + bcol)) ); const half2* h2 = reinterpret_cast<const half2*>(&val); #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < 4; ++j) { *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&smemB[brow][bcol + j * 2]) = h2[j]; } } }; load_b_vec(v0); load_b_vec(v1); __syncthreads(); // Accumulate int n_base = n0 + lane_id * 2; #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < TILE_K; ++k) { half a_val = smemA[warp_id][k]; float fa = __half2float(a_val); half2 b2 = *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&smemB[k][lane_id * 2]); float fb0 = __half2float(__low2half(b2)); // careful, half2 has .x and .y? In cuda_fp16, half2 components can be accessed via .x and .y? Actually, `half2` is a built-in vector type with `.x` and `.y`? No, for `half2` you can use `__low2half` and `__high2half`. // Alternatively use intrinsic __half2float on each component? No, __half2float takes half. // We can do: // half b0 = __low2half(b2); // half b1 = __high2half(b2); // Or reinterpret as array: half b0, b1; // Actually in CUDA C++, half2 has fields x and y if using `half2` struct? Let's check. // According to CUDA docs, half2 has no direct .x but can use __low2half and __high2half. half b_0 = __low2half(b2); half b_1 = __high2half(b2); acc0 += fa * __half2float(b_0); acc1 += fa * __half2float(b_1); } __syncthreads(); } // Write output int b0 = b / D1; int b1 = b % D1; int idx_base = b0 * M * D1 * N + b1 * N; int n = n0 + lane_id * 2; if (m < M && n < N) { D[idx_base + m * D1 * N + n] = __float2half(alpha * acc0); if (n + 1 < N) { D[idx_base + m * D1 * N + (n + 1)] = __float2half(alpha * acc1); } } } ``` Wait, `__low2half` and `__high2half` are correct. If n0 is a multiple of 2 (which it is, n0 = blockIdx.x * 64), and the innermost dimension is N=192, then addresses are contiguous within the warp. However, after n reaches N-1 (191), the next element is b1+1 (same m). For a fixed b0 and m, as n increases, we write along the innermost dimension. TileN=64 means each block processes 64 values of n. But b1 is part of the batch index b! In the 4D tensor shape [B/D1, M, D1, N], a block handling batch b corresponds to a specific b0 and b1. Since b is fixed per block (blockIdx.z), b1 is fixed. So all elements the block writes have the same b1. They only vary in n (innermost). So writes are perfectly contiguous within the n dimension! Great. Wait, blockIdx.z = b ranges from 0 to 95. So b0 = b/12 = 0..7, b1 = b%12 = 0..11. Block processes m0..m0+7 and n0..n0+63. The output elements are in slice (b0, m, b1, n). All have same b0 and b1. n varies within 0..191. So linear address = base + m*D1*N + n. For fixed m and varying n, addresses are contiguous. Within a warp, threads have same m but n varies by 2. So they write to adjacent addresses. Perfect coalescing. This is even better than before! We can allocate D as 4D and return it. So the kernel writes to a flat pointer of a 4D tensor. For H100, we should use `-arch=sm_90` to enable Hopper-specific optimizations. The kernel assumes M is multiple of TILE_M (8) and N is multiple of TILE_N (64). For safety, add checks or handle tail. Since M=384 and N=192 exactly, we can launch grid without checks, but we should keep the bounds checks in writes. Let's write the C++ code: ```cpp #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <torch/extension.h> #define TILE_M 8 #define TILE_N 64 #define TILE_K 64 #define PAD_A 2 #define PAD_B 2 __global__ void __launch_bounds__(256) bmm_perm0213_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const half* __restrict__ B, half* __restrict__ D, int batch, int M, int N, int K, int D1, float alpha ) { int b = blockIdx.z; int m0 = blockIdx.y * TILE_M; int n0 = blockIdx.x * TILE_N; int tid = threadIdx.x; if (b >= batch || m0 >= M || n0 >= N) return; __shared__ __align__(16) half smemA[TILE_M][TILE_K + PAD_A]; __shared__ __align__(16) half smemB[TILE_K][TILE_N + PAD_B]; int warp_id = tid >> 5; int lane_id = tid & 31; int m = m0 + warp_id; float acc0 = 0.0f; float acc1 = 0.0f; for (int k0 = 0; k0 < K; k0 += TILE_K) { // Load A tile: each thread loads 1 half2 (2 elements) int a_tid = tid; int a_row = a_tid >> 5; // 0..7 int a_col = (a_tid & 31) << 1; // 0,2,...,62 if (k0 + a_col < K) { half2 val = *reinterpret_cast<const half2*>( A + ((b * M + (m0 + a_row)) * K + (k0 + a_col)) ); *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&smemA[a_row][a_col]) = val; } // Load B tile: each thread loads 2 float4 (16 elements) int v0 = tid << 1; int v1 = v0 + 1; auto load_b_vec = [&](int vid) { int brow = vid >> 3; // vid / 8 int bcol = (vid & 7) << 3; // (vid % 8) * 8 if (k0 + brow < K && n0 + bcol < N) { float4 val = *reinterpret_cast<const float4*>( B + ((b * K + (k0 + brow)) * N + (n0 + bcol)) ); const half2* h2 = reinterpret_cast<const half2*>(&val); #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < 4; ++j) { *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&smemB[brow][bcol + (j << 1)]) = h2[j]; } } }; load_b_vec(v0); load_b_vec(v1); __syncthreads(); int n = n0 + (lane_id << 1); #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < TILE_K; ++k) { half a_val = smemA[warp_id][k]; float fa = __half2float(a_val); half2 b2 = *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&smemB[k][lane_id << 1]); half b0 = reinterpret_cast<half*>(&b2)[0]; half b1 = reinterpret_cast<half*>(&b2)[1]; acc0 += fa * __half2float(b0); acc1 += fa * __half2float(b1); } __syncthreads(); } int b0 = b / D1; int b1 = b % D1; int idx_base = b0 * M * D1 * N + b1 * N; if (m < M && n < N) { D[idx_base + m * D1 * N + n] = __float2half(alpha * acc0); if (n + 1 < N) { D[idx_base + m * D1 * N + (n + 1)] = __float2half(alpha * acc1); } } } ``` Wait, there might be an issue: `reinterpret_cast<half*>(&b2)[1]` might not work if `half2` has no accessible internal array? But `reinterpret_cast` to `half*` should work because `half2` is just two halfs packed together. However, `half` is a struct/class type in CUDA C++, not a POD? It is a 16-bit type. We can use: ```cuda half b0 = __low2half(b2); half b1 = __high2half(b2); ``` Actually, `__low2half` returns the low half of a half2. Wait, `__low2half` takes a `half2` and returns the first `half`. Yes. Similarly `__high2half`. Let's verify: `__device__ __half __low2half(const __half2 a)`. Yes. So we can use these intrinsics. They are available in `<cuda_fp16.h>`. So: ```cuda half b0 = __low2half(b2); half b1 = __high2half(b2); ``` Also, `b0` and `b1` variables conflict with loop variable names? We'll rename. Let's refine: ```cuda #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < TILE_K; ++k) { half a_val = smemA[warp_id][k]; float fa = __half2float(a_val); half2 b_pair = *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&smemB[k][lane_id << 1]); float fb0 = __half2float(__low2half(b_pair)); float fb1 = __half2float(__high2half(b_pair)); acc0 += fa * fb0; acc1 += fa * fb1; } ``` Make sure we don't conflict with variable `b` (batch). Now, the launcher: ```cpp torch::Tensor bmm_perm0213_cuda(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, int D1, float alpha) { int batch = A.size(0); int M = A.size(1); int K = A.size(2); int N = B.size(2); auto D = torch::empty({batch / D1, M, D1, N}, A.options()); dim3 grid((N + TILE_N - 1) / TILE_N, (M + TILE_M - 1) / TILE_M, batch); dim3 block(256); bmm_perm0213_kernel<<<grid, block>>>( reinterpret_cast<half*>(A.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<half*>(B.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<half*>(D.data_ptr<at::Half>()), batch, M, N, K, D1, alpha ); return D; } ``` Wait, `A.options()` might not work if A is `torch.float16`. `torch::empty(shape, A.options())` uses same dtype and device. Good. We need to handle if `batch % D1 != 0`. In our case it is 0. Final kernel code: ```cuda #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <torch/extension.h> #define TILE_M 8 #define TILE_N 64 #define TILE_K 64 #define PAD_A 2 #define PAD_B 2 __global__ void __launch_bounds__(256) bmm_perm0213_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const half* __restrict__ B, half* __restrict__ D, int batch, int M, int N, int K, int D1, float alpha ) { int b = blockIdx.z; int m0 = blockIdx.y * TILE_M; int n0 = blockIdx.x * TILE_N; int tid = threadIdx.x; if (b >= batch || m0 >= M || n0 >= N) return; __shared__ __align__(16) half smemA[TILE_M][TILE_K + PAD_A]; __shared__ __align__(16) half smemB[TILE_K][TILE_N + PAD_B]; int warp_id = tid >> 5; int lane_id = tid & 31; int m = m0 + warp_id; float acc0 = 0.0f; float acc1 = 0.0f; for (int k0 = 0; k0 < K; k0 += TILE_K) { // Load A tile into smemA int a_tid = tid; int a_row = a_tid >> 5; int n = n0 + (lane_id << 1); #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < TILE_K; ++k) { half a_val = smemA[warp_id][k]; float fa = __half2float(a_val); half2 b_pair = *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&smemB[k][lane_id << 1]); float fb0 = __half2float(__low2half(b_pair)); float fb1 = __half2float(__high2half(b_pair)); acc0 += fa * fb0; acc1 += fa * fb1; } __syncthreads(); } int b0 = b / D1; int b1 = b % D1; int idx_base = b0 * M * D1 * N + b1 * N; int n = n0 + (lane_id << 1); if (m < M && n < N) { half2 out2 = __halves2half2( __float2half(alpha * acc0), __float2half(alpha * acc1) ); D[idx_base + m * D1 * N + n] = __low2half(out2); if (n + 1 < N) { D[idx_base + m * D1 * N + (n + 1)] = __high2half(out2); Kernel outline: ```cuda template<int BM, int BN, int BK, int PAD_A=2, int PAD_B=2> __global__ void bmm_perm0213_kernel( const half* A, const half* B, half* D, int batch, int M, int N, int K, int D1, float alpha ) { __shared__ half smemA[BM][BK + PAD_A]; __shared__ half smemB[BK][BN + PAD_B]; int b = blockIdx.z; int m0 = blockIdx.y * BM; int n0 = blockIdx.x * BN; int tid = threadIdx.x; int warp_id = tid >> 5; int lane_id = tid & 31; // Thread arrangement: 16x16 int thread_m = tid / 16; // 0..15 int thread_n = tid % 16; // 0..15 // Check if this block is valid (it always is for our sizes but check anyway) if (b >= batch || m0 >= M || n0 >= N) return; // Accumulators float acc[4][4]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 4; ++i) for (int j = 0; j < 4; ++j) acc[i][j] = 0.0f; // Main loop over K for (int k0 = 0; k0 < K; k0 += BK) { // Load A tile [BM][BK] from global to shared // 256 threads, each loads 16 halfs = 2 float4 int a_tid = tid; #pragma unroll for (int l = 0; l < 2; ++l) { int offset = a_tid * 16 + l * 8; // 16 halfs per thread, 8 per float4 int row = offset / BK; int col = offset % BK; if (row < BM && k0 + col < K && m0 + row < M) { float4 val = *reinterpret_cast<const float4*>( A + ((b * M + (m0 + row)) * K + (k0 + col)) ); *reinterpret_cast<float4*>(&smemA[row][col]) = val; } } // Load B tile [BK][BN] from global to shared // 256 threads, each loads 16 halfs = 2 float4 int b_tid = tid; #pragma unroll for (int l = 0; l < 2; ++l) { int offset = b_tid * 16 + l * 8; int row = offset / BN; int col = offset % BN; if (row < BK && k0 + row < K && n0 + col < N) { float4 val = *reinterpret_cast<const float4*>( B + ((b * K + (k0 + row)) * N + (n0 + col)) ); *reinterpret_cast<float4*>(&smemB[row][col]) = val; } } __syncthreads(); // Compute tile int base_row = thread_m * 4; int base_col = thread_n * 4; #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < BK; ++k) { float a_vals[4]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 4; ++i) { a_vals[i] = __half2float(smemA[base_row + i][k]); } float b_vals[4]; // Load B row k, cols base_col..base_col+3 as float2 (4 halfs = 8 bytes) float2 b01 = *reinterpret_cast<float2*>(&smemB[k][base_col]); const half2* b_h2 = reinterpret_cast<const half2*>(&b01); b_vals[0] = __half2float(__low2half(b_h2[0])); b_vals[1] = __half2float(__high2half(b_h2[0])); b_vals[2] = __half2float(__low2half(b_h2[1])); b_vals[3] = __half2float(__high2half(b_h2[1])); #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 4; ++i) { float av = a_vals[i]; #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < 4; ++j) { acc[i][j] += av * b_vals[j]; } } } __syncthreads(); } // Write output with permute int b0 = b / D1; int b1 = b % D1; int base_row = thread_m * 4; int base_col = thread_n * 4; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 4; ++i) { int m_out = m0 + base_row + i; if (m_out >= M) continue; int n_out_base = n0 + base_col; if (n_out_base >= N) continue; int idx = b0 * M * D1 * N + m_out * D1 * N + b1 * N + n_out_base; // Produce 4 halfs half2 o0 = __halves2half2( __float2half(alpha * acc[i][0]), __float2half(alpha * acc[i][1]) ); if (n_out_base + 3 < N) *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&D[idx + 2]) = o1; } } ``` Wait, I see an issue with the float2 reinterpretation from smemB. `smemB` is `half smemB[BK][BN+PAD_B]`. `&smemB[k][base_col]` yields `half*`. We can do: threads in a warp are arranged as 8x4. thread_m = tid / 4 (0..7), thread_n = tid % 4 (0..3). That gives 8 rows x 64 cols. 1*16 = 16 elements per thread. Total 512. Now, within a warp: - Threads have unique thread_m (0..7), so they all read DIFFERENT rows of A. - For fixed k, all 32 threads read different rows. But if BK=64, all these addresses are in the same bank! 32-way bank conflict! However, what if the threads read A sequentially or in groups? The compiler might try to load all at once. We can instead have all threads read the same row of A in a sub-loop. Example: for i = 0..7: row = base_row + i a_val = smemA[row][k] // all threads read the same row // broadcast, no conflict! for j (each thread's columns): acc += a_val * b_vals[j] But if each thread accumulates for a different row, then we need to iterate over the 8 rows and each thread picks its own. Not feasible. So we MUST accept some bank conflicts for A, OR add padding to smemA to make banks differ by row. If we add PAD_A=2, BK_eff=66. Then bank depends on row: floor((row*66+k)/2)%32. Consecutive rows differ by 33 words = 1 bank. So 8 different rows map to 8 different banks. For a warp reading 8 different rows simultaneously: no bank conflicts! (assuming no two threads read the exact same bank allocation). Wait, with 32 threads in a warp reading 8 unique rows, only 8 banks are used (one per row). The other 24 banks are idle. No conflict! Beautiful. But we had the alignment issue for loading A with float4 when PAD_A=2. Smaller idea: don't load A with float4. Load with half2. 16 halfs per thread = 8 half2 loads. This is more instructions but avoids the alignment issue. Or, we can use PAD_A=0 but make threads in a warp read a subset of rows that don't conflict. For BK=64, rows 0 and 1 conflict (same bank). Rows 0 and 2 also conflict? floor((0*64+k)/2) = floor(k/2). floor((2*64+k)/2) = 64 + floor(k/2). 64 % 32 = 0. Same bank! Row r maps to bank floor((r*64+k)/2)%32 = (r*32 + floor(k/2))%32 = floor(k/2)%32. So ALL rows map to the same bank for A. With PAD_A=2: bank = floor((r*66+k)/2)%32 = (r*33 + floor(k/2))%32. If a warp handles 32 rows (all different), and assigns each thread to a unique row, then A access is conflict-free! Because each thread touches a different bank. So the winning strategy: - Use smemA with PAD_A=2 (BK=66). - In compute phase, ensure threads within a warp read different rows of A (or at least don't have two threads reading different addresses in the same bank). - Since rows 0..31 use 32 different banks (for fixed k) and rows 32..63 use the same banks as 0..31, we should not have a warp reading from both row <32 and row>=32 simultaneously. Let's use a warp tile of 32 rows x 16 cols. - A warp has 32 threads. Each thread handles 1 row x 16 cols? That's 16 elements. 32 threads * 16 = 512 elements. For r = R, R+1, ..., R+31. The banks are (R*33 + i*33 + floor(k/2)) % 32 for i=0..31. The step is 33 % 32 = 1. So as i goes 0..31, banks step through 32 consecutive values mod 32, covering all banks exactly once. Yes! Perfect, no bank conflicts. For B read: thread reads smemB[k][C..C+15] (16 columns). With BN=64 and no padding (or pad to avoid conflicts in B). Let's check B banks: Bank for smemB[k][c] (reading 16 cols as 4 float2 or 8 half2): With BN=64, byte_addr = (k*64 + c)*2. Bank = floor((k*64+c)/2) % 32 = (k*32 + floor(c/2)) % 32 = floor(c/2) % 32. For a thread reading c=C..C+15, it accesses banks floor(C/2) to floor(C/2)+7. These are 8 consecutive banks. Next thread reads C+16, banks floor(C/2)+8 and floor(C/2)+9. No overlap. Good. So B can use PAD_B=0 (BN=64). Float4 requires 16 bytes, but col_global multiple of 8, not necessarily 16. If k0=0, col=0: col_global=0, aligned. If k0=0, col=8: col_global=8, not divisible by 16. Float4 not aligned. So half2 is the safe choice. So, to summarize the new design: - BM=64, BN=64, BK=64 - PAD_A = 2 (smemA is 64 x 66) - PAD_B = 0 (smemB is 64 x 64) - Block: 256 threads = 8 warps - Warp arrangement: 2 warps along M (each covers 32 rows), 4 warps along N (each covers 16 cols). Actually, writing float4 to shared memory does require 16-byte alignment of the shared memory address. If smemA[row][col] is not 16-byte aligned for odd rows, the store will fault or be slow. We need BK to be multiple of 8 (for vectorized global loads). And we need BK+PAD such that step=(BK+PAD)/2 is coprime to 32. AND (BK+PAD)*2 divisible by 16. Float4 (16 bytes) cannot be aligned for all rows. So indeed: we cannot simultaneously have float4 alignment for all rows of smemA AND have step coprime to 32. We must choose. BANK CONFLICTS ARE WORSE than unaligned float4 stores. Actually, unaligned float4 stores to shared memory are ILLEGAL (will fault). So we cannot use float4 for A smem stores unless we only use it on even rows or something. But that's complicated. The easiest solution: don't use float4 for A shared memory. Just load half2 from global and store half2 to smemA. Since global is aligned (col_global multiple of 8 halfs, which means half2 aligned; actually multiple of 16 halfs = multiple of 32 bytes, better), half2 loads from global are fast. 8 half2 loads per thread is perfectly fine. Similarly for B, float4 works because BN=64 makes row_stride_bytes = 128 = 8*16. All rows are aligned. And bank separation for B is fine because we read along columns within a fixed row. Also, note: we DON'T need coprime bank distribution for B because all threads in a warp read the same row k but different columns. For fixed k, all addresses are in the same row, so bank depends only on column. With BN=64, bank depends on floor(c/2). So as long as threads read different columns, they read different banks (or adjacent banks without conflict if using 64-bit). BN=64's banking scheme is ideal for this: step=32 words = 0 mod 32 for row difference, but since we don't read different rows in B during compute (all threads read row k), it's fine. For A, with BK=66 (PAD=2), bank depends on row. Step=33. Ideal for reading different rows. So the final design is: - smemA: [64][66] half - smemB: [64][64] half - Load A: half2 from global, store half2 to smemA. 8 half2 per thread (16 halfs). - Load B: float4 from global, store float4 to smemB. 2 float4 per thread (16 halfs). - Compute: - Warp tile: 32 rows x 16 cols. - Thread: 1 row x 16 cols. - A read: 1 half per k, no conflict. - B read: 16 halfs per k as 2 float2 or 4 half2. Since all threads in warp read same row k, B accesses are conflict-free. - FMA: 16 FMAs per k. - Unroll BK=64. - Total blocks: 6 (M) * 4 (N, wait N=192, BN=64, so 3) * 96 (batch) = 1728. Wait, 6*3*96 = 1728. Yes. Wait, N=192, BN=64, so N tiles = 3. M=384, BM=64, so M tiles = 6. Total tiles per batch = 18. Total blocks = 18 * 96 = 1728. Threads in warp 1 read smemB[k][16..31] simultaneously. Are these accesses conflicting? For a fixed k, smemB[k][c] bank depends on floor(c/2). Warp 0 accesses c=0..15 -> banks 0..7. Warp 1 accesses c=16..31 -> banks 8..15. Warp 2 accesses c=32..47 -> banks 16..23. Warp 3 accesses c=48..63 -> banks 24..31. Since warps execute independently, but shared memory is a single resource. Can multiple warps execute simultaneously? SM executes one warp at a time per scheduler (4 schedulers per SM on H100). So at any given cycle, shared memory might see requests from 4 warps. If warp 0 and warp 1 are both issued, their requests for banks 0..7 and 8..15 do not overlap. No conflict! Even if they overlap, shared memory on H100 handles limited conflicts. Actually, modern GPUs can handle a certain number of bank conflicts per cycle. But this pattern is ideal. So B reads should be conflict-free across warps as well. For A reads: all warps read different rows. Warp 0 (warp_m=0): rows 0..31. Banks: row*33 + c. Since rows 0..31 step by 33%32=1, they cover all 32 banks. Warp 1 (warp_m=1): rows 32..63. Banks: 32*33 + r*33 + c = (r+32)*33 + c. Mod 32: r*33 + c mod 32. Since 32*33 = 1056, 1056%32=0. So rows 32..63 use the same banks as rows 0..31. But warp 0 and warp 1 are executing independently. If they happen to execute on different schedulers simultaneously, their row accesses to smemA might target the same banks but with different addresses. This could still be okay because the memory array has multiple banks. Wait, if both warps request bank 0 at different addresses, it's a conflict for that bank. However, does H100 shared memory handle multiple requests to the same bank? It serializes them. So if warp 0 and warp 1 both hit bank 0 (which they will, since rows 0 and 32 both map to bank c), then that's a conflict. But wait! In the compute phase, we have: for k in 0..63: smemA[...][k] smemB[...][k] For warp 0: row r. Bank = (r*33 + floor(k/2)) % 32. For warp 1: row r+32. Bank = ((r+32)*33 + floor(k/2)) % 32 = (r*33 + 1056 + floor(k/2)) % 32 = (r*33 + floor(k/2)) % 32. Same bank! So rows 0 and 32, 1 and 33, etc. are in the same bank. This means when warp 0 (rows 0..31) and warp 1 (rows 32..63) execute simultaneously and access smemA, there could be 2-way bank conflicts for every request pair that hits the same bank. Can we avoid this? We could rearrange the warp schedule or compute phase. But warps execute asynchronously. However, if the block has enough warps, the SM might schedule them on different cycles. But since the loop body is identical for all warps, they might stay in sync, causing conflicts. To truly avoid this, we need the bank mapping for rows 32..63 to be different from rows 0..31. With step=33, after 32 rows, the bank repeats. So row r and r+32 always conflict. This means we should only have at most 32 unique rows accessed simultaneously per block? Or we need a different PAD. What if we use PAD such that step mod 32 doesn't repeat for the full range of 64 rows? Bank(r) = (r * step) % 32. We want this to be distinct for r=0..63. But modulo 32, there are only 32 values. By pigeonhole principle, 64 rows must share banks. Any choice of step will repeat after 32 rows. So having 64 rows in a block inevitably means row r and r+32 share a bank. WMMA design: - Use `nvcuda::wmma` for 16x16x16 fragments. - Block size: 256 threads = 8 warps. - Each warp computes one 16x16 tile using mma. - 8 warps per block compute 8 tiles. - To cover 32x64 block: 2x4 tiles = 8 tiles. WMMA details: - `wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, 16, 16, 16, half, wmma::row_major> a_frag;` - `wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_b, 16, 16, 16, half, wmma::row_major> b_frag;` - `wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, 16, 16, 16, float> acc_frag;` Each warp loads one A frag (16x16) and one B frag (16x16). The M dimension of the block is 32, so 2 warps along M. N dimension is 64, so 4 warps along N. Total 2*4=8 warps. For the compute phase: - For k_tile = 0..BK-1 in steps of 16 (because wmma k-dim is 16): - Load A frag from smemA for warp's M slice and current K slice. - Load B frag from smemB for current K slice and warp's N slice. - mma_sync. BK=64. K=384. K tile iterations: 384/64 = 6 outer, 64/16 = 4 inner per BK tile. Total 24 mma instructions per warp per output tile. This is much cleaner to write and should perform very well because it uses Tensor Cores directly. H100 has very fast WMMA throughput (though direct mma.sync is slightly faster, wmma is still good and much simpler). Let me write the WMMA kernel. Block tile: BM=32, BN=64, BK=64. Warps: 8 warps arranged as 2x4. warp_m = warp_id / 4 (0..1) warp_n = warp_id % 4 (0..3) Each warp computes a 16x16 tile at: m_base = warp_m * 16 n_base = warp_n * 16 smemA: [BM][BK+PAD_A] = [32][66]. WMMA fragment for A (16x16 row major) loads from address with given leading dimension. The address is the start of the 16x16 tile. For A, tile starts at smemA[warp_m*16][k_inner]. With row stride 128 bytes, consecutive rows are 128 bytes apart, which is a multiple of 32 banks * 4 bytes = 128 bytes. So each row starts at the same bank. For ldmatrix, which loads a 16x16 fragment from shared memory, this causes severe bank conflicts because all 8 rows (or however many) start at the same bank offset. Actually, CUTLASS and other libraries use shared memory swizzling to avoid bank conflicts for WMMA. bank conflicts for ldmatrix are a real issue. To avoid them, the matrix rows must be offset in banks. This is exactly why swizzling and padding are used. For Ampere/Hopper, `cp.async` with `cute::Swizzle` is used. Final design: - BM=32, BN=64, BK=64 - smemA: [32][66] (padded by 2 to avoid bank conflicts) - smemB: [64][64] (no padding needed, row stride=128 bytes=32 banks, perfect for column reads since all threads read from same row k but different columns) - 256 threads - 8 warps arranged 1x8 (all warps cover full 32 rows, each handles 8 columns) - Thread in warp: lane_id=0..31 -> row = lane_id (0..31). base_col = warp_id * 8 (0..56). - Each thread computes 1 row x 8 cols = 8 outputs. - Load A: 256 threads cover 32*64=2048 halfs. They come from 4 rows, each row having 8 threads loading 64 contiguous halfs. Each row load can be merged into 1 or 2 transactions. Very efficient. For smemA store: tid=0 writes to row 0, cols 0..7. Now, writing the code: ```python CUDA_SOURCE = r''' #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <torch/extension.h> #define BM 32 #define BN 64 #define BK 64 #define PAD_A 2 __global__ void __launch_bounds__(256) bmm_perm0213_kernel( const at::Half* __restrict__ A_ptr, const at::Half* __restrict__ B_ptr, at::Half* __restrict__ D_ptr, int batch, int M, int N, int K, int D1, float alpha ) { using half = __half; const half* __restrict__ A = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(A_ptr); const half* __restrict__ B = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(B_ptr); half* __restrict__ D = reinterpret_cast<half*>(D_ptr); int b = blockIdx.z; int m0 = blockIdx.y * BM; int n0 = blockIdx.x * BN; int tid = threadIdx.x; if (b >= batch || m0 >= M || n0 >= N) return; __shared__ __align__(16) half smemA[BM][BK + PAD_A]; __shared__ __align__(16) half smemB[BK][BN]; int warp_id = tid >> 5; int lane_id = tid & 31; int row = lane_id; // 0..31 within block tile int base_col = warp_id * 8; // 0,8,16,...,56 // Initialize accumulators float acc[8]; #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < 8; ++j) acc[j] = 0.0f; // Main loop over K for (int k0 = 0; k0 < K; k0 += BK) { // Load A tile: each thread loads 8 halfs (4 half2) into smemA #pragma unroll for (int l = 0; l < 4; ++l) { int local_tid = tid * 8 + l * 2; // half2 index within tile (2 halfs) int a_row = local_tid >> 6; // /64 int a_col = local_tid & 63; // %64 if (m0 + a_row < M && k0 + a_col < K) { half2 val = *reinterpret_cast<const half2*>( A + ((b * M + (m0 + a_row)) * K + (k0 + a_col)) ); *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&smemA[a_row][a_col]) = val; } else { smemA[a_row][a_col] = half(); smemA[a_row][a_col + 1] = half(); } } // Load B tile: each thread loads 16 halfs (2 float4) into smemB #pragma unroll for (int l = 0; l < 2; ++l) { int local_tid = tid * 16 + l * 8; // 8 halfs per float4 int b_row = local_tid >> 6; // /64 int b_col = local_tid & 63; // %64 if (k0 + b_row < K && n0 + b_col < N) { float4 val = *reinterpret_cast<const float4*>( B + ((b * K + (k0 + b_row)) * N + (n0 + b_col)) ); *reinterpret_cast<float4*>(&smemB[b_row][b_col]) = val; } else { // zero out shared memory *reinterpret_cast<float4*>(&smemB[b_row][b_col]) = make_float4(0.f, 0.f, 0.f, 0.f); } } __syncthreads(); // Compute 32x64 tile #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < BK; ++k) { float a_val = __half2float(smemA[row][k]); float4 b4 = *reinterpret_cast<float4*>(&smemB[k][base_col]); half2 b01 = reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&b4)[0]; half2 b23 = reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&b4)[1]; half2 b45 = reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&b4)[2]; half2 b67 = reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&b4)[3]; float b_vals[8]; b_vals[0] = __half2float(__low2half(b01)); b_vals[1] = __half2float(__high2half(b01)); b_vals[2] = __half2float(__low2half(b23)); b_vals[3] = __half2float(__high2half(b23)); b_vals[4] = __half2float(__low2half(b45)); b_vals[5] = __half2float(__high2half(b45)); b_vals[6] = __half2float(__low2half(b67)); b_vals[7] = __half2float(__high2half(b67)); #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < 8; ++j) { acc[j] += a_val * b_vals[j]; } } __syncthreads(); } // Write output with permute [batch/D1, M, D1, N] int m_out = m0 + row; if (m_out >= M) return; int n_out_base = n0 + base_col; if (n_out_base >= N) return; int b0 = b / D1; int b1 = b % D1; int64_t idx_base = (int64_t)b0 * M * D1 * N + (int64_t)m_out * D1 * N + (int64_t)b1 * N + n_out_base; // Store 8 halfs (4 half2) #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < 4; ++j) { half2 out = __halves2half2( __float2half(alpha * acc[j * 2]), __float2half(alpha * acc[j * 2 + 1]) ); int64_t idx = idx_base + j * 2; if (n_out_base + j * 2 + 1 < N) { *reinterpret_cast<half2*>(&D[idx]) = out; } else if (n_out_base + j * 2 < N) { D[idx] = __float2half(alpha * acc[j * 2]); } } } torch::Tensor bmm_perm0213_cuda(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, int D1, float alpha) { int batch = A.size(0); int M = A.size(1); int K = A.size(2); int N = B.size(2); auto D = torch::empty({batch / D1, M, D1, N}, A.options()); dim3 grid((N + BN - 1) / BN, (M + BM - 1) / BM, batch); dim3 block(256); bmm_perm0213_kernel<<<grid, block>>>( A.data_ptr<at::Half>(), B.data_ptr<at::Half>(), D.data_ptr<at::Half>(), batch, M, N, K, D1, alpha ); return D; } ''' ``` Wait, I need to include a check that batch is divisible by D1.
